> Accepted finding #7 (Moderate) from the @ZCash campaign.
>
> The root cause was inaccurate threat modelling of the actors involved and relying on what those actors should do instead of what they COULD do.
>
> At @bluethroat_labs, "Assumptions Break Systems" is an internalised doctrine. [pic.x.com/oAgLNOXF4r] https://twitter.com/saxenism/status/2118554252570427494

    > Bounty #10 on @immunefi stems from the same root as #9.
    >
    > Our in-house agents, specialising in low-level execution & consensus flaws were leveraged by our experts to find this vulnerability.
    >
    > We were able to break the guarantees of deterministic execution by fiddling with the exact CPU instructions for the same contracts.
    >
    > Overall, it was really fun. Onto the next one :)

> "Just a .pem in a public repo" is the trigger for sure, just not the bug.
>
> This attack class is literally the first thing discussed in the "Dumb Ways to Get Rekt w/ TEEs" section of the TEE Security Handbook from @bluethroat_labs.
>
> -----
>
> What actually happened:
>
> The committed https://t.co/oBrYn0YFyn

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @1041276721759043584 — 2026-06-22 13:12 UTC (2026-06-22 14:12 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/1041276721759043584/status/2069045986236695020
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 179, impression 190606, like 350, quote 49, reply 41, retweet 49
    
    > 🚨Taiko drained for ~$1.7M. Root cause: a private key committed to a public GitHub repo.
    >
    > enclave-key.pem, the RSA key used to sign all of Taiko's SGX enclaves, sat in the public taikoxyz/raiko repo. That key is the whole trust model.
    >
    > The attacker derived MrSigner from the public key, signed their own malicious enclave with the leaked key, and registered as a trusted prover. The L1 contracts trust any enclave whose MrSigner matches. It matched.
    >
    > From there: forged SGX attestations on fake L2 blocks, processMessage() sets the message to RETRIABLE, retryMessage() does zero proof verification, funds leave.
    >
    > No key theft. No social engineering. No SGX exploit. Just a .pem in a public repo.
    >
    > Good opportunity to recall that SGX is broken. But here, nobody even had to break it. 
    >
    > It's just yet another key management failure. The whole system was only ever as strong as the secrecy of one RSA key, and that secrecy depended on a human not running git add . on the wrong folder. AI greps every commit of every public repo at machine speed. Assume that is already happening.
    >
    > The only real exit: a verifier that checks a succinct validity proof of the L2 state transition. It trusts no enclave, no MrSigner, no operator discipline. It checks the math. In that world this exact attack becomes cryptographically impossible rather than operationally unlikely, because there is no privileged key whose leak forges the entire system. There is just a proof.
    >
    > Stay safe.

> - Vidur for TEE systems

> It is more like the agent got better https://t.co/BBleSckicd https://t.co/6gj1asn9Ka

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HK_mSx1a4AAeSig.jpg — no alt text provided by author

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @19628766 — 2026-06-15 20:39 UTC (2026-06-15 21:39 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/19628766/status/2066621765028327922
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1917, impression 88421, like 1152, quote 17, reply 66, retweet 166
    
    > 🚨How do you index the entire Linux kernel (28M lines of code) for an AI agent in 3 minutes?
    >
    > You stop letting the agent read files one by one.
    >
    > There is a fascinating new open-source release called codebase-memory-mcp.
    >
    > It's a code intelligence engine that swaps traditional file-searching for high-speed AST knowledge graphs.
    >
    > What makes this project stand out is the research behind it.
    >
    > Evaluated across 31 real-world repositories (detailed in arXiv:2603.27277), the architectural shift yields massive efficiency gains:
    > → 99% reduction in tokens for structural queries
    > → 83% answer quality across complex tasks
    > → 2.1x fewer tool calls required
    >
    > It maps functions, classes, HTTP routes, and cross-service links into a graph. When the agent needs context, it queries the graph directly.
    >
    > Security is prioritized too: everything happens 100% locally on your machine via a single static binary.
    >
    > It runs entirely locally.
    >
    > No Docker, no Ollama, no API keys.
    >
    > You download the binary, restart your agent, and it just works.
    >
    > Are we one good index away from cutting AI dev costs to zero?
    >
    > Paper and Repo links in the thread ↓

> 3/10 What `codebase-memory-mcp` changes is simple:
>
> It builds a persistent structural memory of the repo.
>
> So instead of re-reading files one by one every time, the agent can query a graph of:
>
> - files
> - functions
> - methods
> - symbols
> - snippets
> - callers/callees
> - relationships
>
> Not exactly a language server, but similar in spirit: repo structure becomes queryable instead of being repeatedly reloaded into context.

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @1675284992853700609 — 2026-05-27 14:07 UTC (2026-05-27 15:07 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/1675284992853700609/status/2059637607403831732
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 9104, impression 3320856, like 52184, quote 1075, reply 1358, retweet 13319
    
    > CEOs are quietly realizing the AI replacement plan has a problem.
    > Two problems, actually.
    >
    > One: the token costs for running AI agents are now exceeding what they were paying the employees they fired.
    >
    > Two: when the tokens run out, the AI stops. Just stops. No continuity. No workaround. Just a spinning wheel where your workforce used to be.
    >
    > You fired humans to save money and bought a subscription that bills you into a corner.
    >
    > The employees you let go knew what to do when things broke.
    > The AI just invoices you for the outage.
    >
    > And then there’s the permission problem nobody wants to talk about.
    >
    > To do its job, the AI agent needs access. Full access. Your systems, your patents, your contracts, your future plans. Everything you spent years building, handed over to a process that has no loyalty, no discretion, and no skin in the game.
    >
    > You didn’t hire a replacement.
    > You gave a stranger with no soul the keys to everything you own. 
    >
    > Enjoy.

    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 54, impression 18052, like 91, quote 11, reply 24, retweet 4
    
    > Atomic transactions are over. DeFi needs delayed settlement to survive. Without it, the risk/reward is hard.
    >
    > So alongside its public release, Royco will introduce delayed settlement. Instead of every transaction happening instantly, it enters a delayed queue. Initially, that delay will be 24 hours. 
    >
    > On one hand, a t+1 settlement means the system will take longer to grow, and rates won't be as reactive. But on the other hand, the ability to keep depositors safe remains the top priority - and this is a strong lever to do so. 
    >
    > Delayed settlement joins other key security practices at Royco, including formal verification, traditional audits, active monitoring, and more. More on the 8 layers of security in the next tweet.

> Another day, another hack.

>

>
> Monitoring, circuit breakers, staggered withdrawals, and tx analysis, etc need to be implemented YESTERDAY. https://t.co/PkL75nKoGk

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @1981269826497892352 — 2026-04-27 09:40 UTC (2026-04-27 10:40 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/1981269826497892352/status/2048698708309705069
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 41, impression 14208, like 49, quote 14, reply 5, retweet 3
    
    > 🚨 @Singularity_Fi  - Loss ~$413K (2026-04-26)
    >
    > Type: Oracle Misconfiguration / Share Inflation
    >
    > The dynBaseUSDCv3 vault on Base prices its non-USDC reserves via UniswapV3Oracle. On 2026-01-19 the protocol admin registered the six yield-token oracle routes with a Uniswap V3 fee tier of 42. Uniswap V3 only enables fee tiers 100/500/3000/10000, so factory.getPool(USDC, X, 42) returns address(0) for every token - silently killing the direct price path. The WETH-fallback pools that did exist had zero liquidity, as a result VaultTokensLib.totalAssets() only counted the ~$100 idle USDC. 
    >
    > The attacker flash-loaned 100k USDC from Morpho, deposited into the vault to mint ~99.99% of supply at the broken ratio, then redeemed the tokens to get a proportional cut of every actual token balance independently of the oracle, draining the underlying yield tokens.
    >
    > This is a preliminary research, the official post-mortem to follow as per announcement in Telegram: https://t.me/Singularity_Fi/262366
    >
    > TX: https://basescan.org/tx/0x00b949bc3ed3edb58b04faedfbd8eb1db2edceae761382e80fe012919f8d3732
    > Victim: https://basescan.org/address/0x67b93f6676bd1911c5fae7ffa90fff5f35e14dcd
    > Oracle: https://basescan.org/address/0x73b8c192bfc323c3ea224c88219d55dfc319e89f
    > setUniV3fee=42: https://basescan.org/tx/0x2df0be7a17bd69a2f732c1396796690240aecdfaf13b0a8f60f49f95a8dbe150

## 2026-04-17 · Post · Well, I have been using AI rigorously for the past few months. Almost daily.


Tags (automatic): AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @saxenism — 2026-04-17 12:37 UTC (2026-04-17 13:37 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2045119506293588044
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 182, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Well, I have been using AI rigorously for the past few months. Almost daily.
>
> And I have more or less arrived at the same conclusion: the current AI wave is most definitely a bubble.
>
> What stops more people from calling it that is the fact that it *does* have legitimate use cases. And it's hype to substance ratio is not as high as that for NFTs.
>
> However, from my experience, at least in the software domain, if you really want to use AI in a production-grade setting, and really want to let “AI handle everything,” you will end up re-inventing “Computer Science 101” all over again, except now with a whole new class of problems.

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/Jeyffre/status/2045093815346958823

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @192462804 — 2026-04-17 10:55 UTC (2026-04-17 11:55 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/192462804/status/2045093815346958823
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 2381, like 47, quote 1, reply 4, retweet 4
    
    > The parallels between NFTs of 2021 and AI of 2026 are hard not to notice.
    >
    > - People brag about "token" spending
    >
    > - You buy in, and then get rugged (the model gets nerfed)
    >
    > - There's an almost religious need to "onboard" people to the new technology. Maybe I should remove the "almost" qualifier...
    >
    > - The tech has some legitimately good usecases, but it's also forced into usecases where every reasonable person knows it doesn't belong
    >
    > - Both booms depend trillion dollar capital injections (cov** stimulus and trillion-dollar AI spending)
    >
    > - Gatekeeping/whitelist/artificial demand gen for "the best opportunities" (models "too good to release")
    >
    > - People worried they'll be poor if they miss the "generational opportunity"

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2034595139700871314
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 808, like 5, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> Can someone help me w/ the said coupons?
>
> I have a new account on HackenProof, and I promise not to plaster AI slop on any bounty I participate in.
>
> Who can I reach out to? https://t.co/wreGTFiFpo

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @4241393403 — 2026-03-18 16:06 UTC (2026-03-18 17:06 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/4241393403/status/2034300493078413491
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 24, impression 25869, like 129, quote 10, reply 16, retweet 12
    
    > Paid submissions? Let’s talk
    > We need to be honest about what’s happening to bug bounty right now
    >
    > We live in AI era, where submission volume is growing fast, but signal is not
    > A lot of reports getting lost, delayed, or stuck in review loops
    >
    > And this hurts everyone - especially professional whitehats with real findings
    >
    > Over the last months, we’ve been trying to fix this step by step
    >
    > Reputation points system was first
    > you submit spam → you get penalty points → you lose ability to submit
    > simple incentive on quality
    >
    > Then - MCP
    > Which helps teams triage faster, identify duplicates, reduce review time. Many companies already using it.
    >
    > And now we are introducing a new option - submission fees. We’ve been hearing this request from many companies and honestly, it feels like a next logical step to make the game more fair for everyone.
    >
    > This is optional, not default, and not something every company will enable.
    > Fees going to be small ($1-$5), so this is not about monetization too
    >
    > This is about adding a bit of friction, so people think twice before submitting something they are not confident in
    > Because today, there is almost no downside to spam. With $20 subscription, any user can generate thousands of reports even without understanding of them.
    >
    > At the same time, we fully understand concerns, whitehats are our biggest asset and we still want new researchers to join the space, so we added:
    > • free credits for new users (via coupons)
    > • support for high-signal researchers 
    >
    > Goal is very simple - improve signal without losing important reports
    >
    > I will keep you in a loop once any of HackenProof clients will enable it
    >
    > Lets fix bug bounty together

> Compromised and revoked TEE machines could pass dstack's attestation verification as perfectly valid, due to missing checks.

>

> + $2,500 in bounty offered
> + disclosure timelines framed as "threat"
> + wiped shared Notion
> + severities downgraded in a public blog post
>
> Here's the full story:  🧵👇🏻

> Daily reminder for protocols to make "monitoring" (both on-chain and off-chain) a first-class citizen of their "security" stack. https://t.co/GvtWdhn4NX

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @saxenism — 2026-02-09 13:53 UTC (2026-02-09 14:53 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2020858473161031764
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1305, like 7, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 0
    
    > This is a take I strongly align with, because it clearly identifies why security is such a big deal in Web3.
    >
    > You cannot afford to "experiment" with people's hard-earned money. Period.
    >
    > ---
    >
    > That said, the downstream impact on smaller teams is real, and it shouldn't be brushed https://t.co/ftozHVH9KH
    
    **Quoted post (full text):**
    
        #### Quoted post — @1261220379193991169 — 2026-02-07 08:51 UTC (2026-02-07 09:51 WAT)
        
        - URL: https://x.com/1261220379193991169/status/2020057795522097464
        - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 40, impression 40988, like 152, quote 15, reply 45, retweet 12
        
        > Some audit bros are on my ass, so here’s the full explanation:
        >
        > In other areas of tech, experimentation is very easy and cheap. You can launch projects, test demand, see what sticks, and shut down what doesn't. The cost of learning whether an idea is worth pursuing is very low. 
        >
        > This encourages speed, iteration, and risk-taking. Most projects fail, and that’s fine, because failure is affordable when it comes at a low cost.
        >
        > In crypto, experimentation is expensive from day one. 
        >
        > The moment a product touches user funds, expectations jump straight to production grade security. No one wants to interact with a codebase that hasn't been audited, often multiple times. This is rational behavior because the stakes are high.
        >
        > As a result, crypto ends up biased towards teams that already have capital. Smaller and bootstrapped teams don't launch at all or they take shortcuts that increase risk, neither of which is healthy.
        >  
        > This raises the barrier to entry and filters out a huge amount of projects. Many ideas never get tested and we lose out on useful potential projects.
        >
        > If crypto wants more innovation, it needs to make it cheaper to fail early.
        >
        > Right now, failure is too expensive, and that’s one of the biggest reasons experimentation in crypto lags behind other major technology sectors.
        
        **Links in post:** https://twitter.com/abbaskhan/status/2019876281035485488
        
        **Quoted post (full text):**
        
            #### Quoted post — @1261220379193991169 — 2026-02-06 20:50 UTC (2026-02-06 21:50 WAT)
            
            - URL: https://x.com/1261220379193991169/status/2019876281035485488
            - Metrics (at fetch): not returned
            
            > One of the biggest reasons why it's hard to experiment in crypto is security audits and their costs.
            >
            > I've spoken to more than 10 teams in the last month who are all currently ready to launch on mainnet, but are held back by audits and their insane cost.
            >
            > A basic audit can cost up to 50k for a small codebase, which makes it hard for bootstrapped projects to launch and explore if they should even be spending their time on this.
            >
            > The industry did a terrible job of overpricing security audits and it has strongly held the space back.

    >
    > In crypto, experimentation is expensive from day one. 
    >
    > The moment a product touches user funds, expectations jump straight to production grade security. No one wants to interact with a codebase that hasn't been audited, often multiple times. This is rational behavior because the stakes are high.
    >
    > As a result, crypto ends up biased towards teams that already have capital. Smaller and bootstrapped teams don't launch at all or they take shortcuts that increase risk, neither of which is healthy.
    >  
    > This raises the barrier to entry and filters out a huge amount of projects. Many ideas never get tested and we lose out on useful potential projects.
    >
    > If crypto wants more innovation, it needs to make it cheaper to fail early.
    >
    > Right now, failure is too expensive, and that’s one of the biggest reasons experimentation in crypto lags behind other major technology sectors.
    
    **Links in post:** https://twitter.com/abbaskhan/status/2019876281035485488
    
    **Quoted post (full text):**
    
        #### Quoted post — @1261220379193991169 — 2026-02-06 20:50 UTC (2026-02-06 21:50 WAT)
        
        - URL: https://x.com/1261220379193991169/status/2019876281035485488
        - Metrics (at fetch): not returned
        
        > One of the biggest reasons why it's hard to experiment in crypto is security audits and their costs.
        >
        > I've spoken to more than 10 teams in the last month who are all currently ready to launch on mainnet, but are held back by audits and their insane cost.
        >
        > A basic audit can cost up to 50k for a small codebase, which makes it hard for bootstrapped projects to launch and explore if they should even be spending their time on this.
        >
        > The industry did a terrible job of overpricing security audits and it has strongly held the space back.

    #### Quoted post — @279687067 — 2026-01-20 10:23 UTC (2026-01-20 11:23 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/279687067/status/2013558068529836129
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 4, impression 1732, like 33, quote 1, reply 6, retweet 0
    
    > Defense-in-depth is one of the most underrated ideas in security. Even more so in DeFi.
    >
    > The core mindset is simple - assume any single control can fail.
    >
    > So you add layers. If one breaks, another is still there to catch it.
    >
    > In a world of leaked keys, novel attack vectors and human error, one line of defense simply does not cut it.

> Let me get into the weeds here.
>
> Here's a realistic picture of what I really meant when I said the verification **burden** has been shifted.
>
> Context: In modern SGX/TDX TEE protocols, the PROVER sends:
> + attestation evidence (quote/report)
> + verification collateral needed to https://t.co/Kn0lcm2Qph https://t.co/pqVhJ4nzeS

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/G_GglESWcAAaBT4.jpg — no alt text provided by author

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @saxenism — 2026-01-19 09:46 UTC (2026-01-19 10:46 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2013186167974572348
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 4, impression 1600, like 16, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 3
    
    > Intel removed itself as the bottleneck in online verification paths when it started moving away from (EPID quotes + IAS verification) to (DCAP collateral + local verification).
    >
    > It gave data centre customers more control of attestation, but also put the burden of correct verification on verifiers.
    >
    > Now, the correctness of attestation is only as good as the verifier implementation.
    >
    > I wonder if that was the right move.
    >
    > ----
    >
    > For context, these are the EPID and DCAP schemes:

## 2026-01-19 · Thread · 1. If you're building with SGX, DO NOT let your "software mode" become a production footgun.


Tags (automatic): Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge, TEE / enclave, AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @saxenism — 2026-01-19 07:09 UTC (2026-01-19 08:09 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2013146724269953380
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 5, impression 323, like 10, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 2

> 1. If you're building with SGX, DO NOT let your "software mode" become a production footgun.
>
> A single misconfigured env variable can result in your entire attestation being bypassed. 
>
> I've seen this pattern way too many times during my security research 🦧


### Thread part 2 — @saxenism — 2026-01-19 07:09 UTC (2026-01-19 08:09 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2013146726673113568
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 144, like 3, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 2. The classic mistake:
>
> `return strings.ToUpper(os.Getenv("SGX_MODE")) != "SW"`
>
> Unset var --&gt; SW mode --&gt; verification skipped
>
> Flip the logic:
>
> `return os.Getenv("SGX_MODE") != "SW"`
>
> Now unset = SAFE path. 
> Secure by default, always.


### Thread part 3 — @saxenism — 2026-01-19 07:09 UTC (2026-01-19 08:09 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2013146729143570604
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 130, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 3. Runtime env vars WILL get misconfigured. Enforce HW mode at build time:
> ```
> SGX_MODE ?= HW  # default to HW
>
> build-mainnet:
>     --build-arg SGX_MODE=HW  # explicit, no ambiguity
> ```
> If it's not in the Makefile AND your CI pipeline, it's still a ticking time bomb for you.


### Thread part 4 — @saxenism — 2026-01-19 07:09 UTC (2026-01-19 08:09 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2013146731941433637
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 30, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 4. Always follow Defense in Depth.
> Never a single point of bypass:
>
> Example:
> Go verification (bypassed in SW)
>              |
> Enclave verification (catches it)
>              |
> Consensus layer (2/3+ validators catch it)
>
> One fails? Others catch it. That's the whole point.


### Thread part 5 — @saxenism — 2026-01-19 07:09 UTC (2026-01-19 08:09 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/2013146734315364824
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 27, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 5. Name unsafe shit UNSAFE.
>
> ```
> func VerifyRaCert()        // the real deal
> func UNSAFE_VerifyRaCert() // CLI-only, skips verification
> ```
>
> This saves your auditors SO much time. They can weed out false positives quickly and spend that time on the REAL vulnerabilities in your

>
> ``` 
> rg -i "sgx_mode|software.?mode|simulation" 
> rg -i "skip.*verif|bypass|!.*hardware" 
> rg "UNSAFE_|unsafe_"
> ```
>

## 2025-12-13 · Post · Again, designing around availability for TEE-based protocols is a MUST. https://twitter.com/saxenism


Tags (automatic): TEE / enclave


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-12-13 16:03 UTC (2025-12-13 17:03 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1999872692170990022
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 559, like 6, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> Again, designing around availability for TEE-based protocols is a MUST. https://twitter.com/saxenism/status/1999748628899069997

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/saxenism/status/1999748628899069997

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @saxenism — 2025-12-13 07:50 UTC (2025-12-13 08:50 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1999748628899069997
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 675, like 2, quote 1, reply 0, retweet 1
    
    > Agreed, it's builder-focused, that's the audience. And users may tolerate downtime if the benefits are clear. My point isn't about going for high-nine SLAs.
    >
    > The difference is architectural: when a normal service fails, you can often degrade gracefully. When a TEE providing privacy or confidentiality fails, graceful degradation often means leaking exactly what you were protecting.
    >
    > This isn’t a 4-nines problem; it’s a design problem. Protocols need to be designed so that temporary unavailability doesn’t force unsafe fallbacks or leave them in irrecoverable states.
    >
    > It’s also worth expanding the threat model beyond “TEE provider has an outage” to “TEE provider is adversarial and makes instances unavailable at the worst possible moment.” Availability failures become DoS vectors when the attacker controls the host.

## 2025-12-12 · Thread · A critical systemic risk that I see missing from TEE-heavy protocol architectures is: fallbacks for 


Tags (automatic): TEE / enclave


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-12-12 05:46 UTC (2025-12-12 06:46 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1999355134451810809
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 5, impression 2227, like 22, quote 1, reply 7, retweet 3

> A critical systemic risk that I see missing from TEE-heavy protocol architectures is: fallbacks for availability.
>
> TEEs only provide guarantees of confidentiality &amp; integrity, but NOT of availability.
>
> The Host OS can:
> +  just stop scheduling enclave threads
> + SIGKILL the process


### Thread part 2 — @saxenism — 2025-12-13 07:50 UTC (2025-12-13 08:50 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1999748628899069997
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 675, like 2, quote 1, reply 0, retweet 1

> Agreed, it's builder-focused, that's the audience. And users may tolerate downtime if the benefits are clear. My point isn't about going for high-nine SLAs.
>
> The difference is architectural: when a normal service fails, you can often degrade gracefully. When a TEE providing privacy or confidentiality fails, graceful degradation often means leaking exactly what you were protecting.
>
> This isn’t a 4-nines problem; it’s a design problem. Protocols need to be designed so that temporary unavailability doesn’t force unsafe fallbacks or leave them in irrecoverable states.
>
> It’s also worth expanding the threat model beyond “TEE provider has an outage” to “TEE provider is adversarial and makes instances unavailable at the worst possible moment.” Availability failures become DoS vectors when the attacker controls the host.

## 2025-12-11 · Post · If you talk to someone about investing, let's say, gold, and they actually understand it, the conver


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-12-11 11:51 UTC (2025-12-11 12:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1999084535699603678
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 638, like 5, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> If you talk to someone about investing, let's say, gold, and they actually understand it, the conversation goes into holding periods, historical returns, geopolitics, arbitrage, and comparisons with stocks or real estate.
>
> But if the person has zero context and still wants to talk on equal footing, they start saying things like:
> “Why gold? Why not platinum or emerald? What if ocean-mined gold floods the market? What if asteroid mining becomes feasible? What if artificial gold shows up?”
>
> All viable scenarios, but come the fuck on. If these scenarios become real, we have much greater problems at hands already.
>
> This is exactly what talking about TEEs feels like sometimes.
>
> TEEs, like gold, are not perfect. I admit that openly and I actively advocate building mitigations around their weaknesses.
>
> But some people just want to say something ostensibly smart and walk off with a smirk.

> A TEE is an interesting piece of technology, with its own set of security guarantees under a given threat model.

### Thread part 7 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931423173734859
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 168, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 6)
> Problem Statement:  Exploring can TEEs be used to provide privacy to users in a permissionless chain?
>
> Immediate Problem: For any public blockchain, public verifiability is a MUST. By default, the nodes of this TEE blockchain network will be processing the transactions...


### Thread part 8 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931429263888717
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 159, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 7)
> via the blockchain VM inside TEEs which will encrypt everything by default. Hence none of these network nodes can share data of these txns with a full node so that anyone who run a full node can replay all transactions and be assured of the correctness of the blockchain state


### Thread part 9 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931431654588440
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 169, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> 8)
> Proposed Solution:
>
> • TEE attestations for privacy
> • ZK proofs (arguments of knowledge) for verifiability
> • Governance for security edge cases not handled by the above combination


### Thread part 10 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931434095620109
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 167, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 9)
> 1. ZK Proofs (arguments of knowledge) serve two main purposes:
> - Introduces verifiability in the network
> - The main issue with “TeeDotFail breaking TEEs” is that TEEs can basically attest to anything and thereby include invalid transactions in the blocks.


### Thread part 11 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931442257817966
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 150, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 10)
> -- Well, if the node can produce the attestation for a particular block but is unable to produce the ZK Proof for that block, then that is a pretty good indicator that a particular TEE node has been compromised.


### Thread part 12 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931444786983351
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 139, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 11)
> -- Anyone can verify the state of the blockchain based on the info posted by the node, ie, initial state, ZK proof of state transition and final state.
>
> 2. Would chain performance deteriorate since nodes would have to process transactions inside TEEs &amp; generate proofs too?


### Thread part 13 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931452101849219
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 129, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 12)
> Not really.
> For a given node, “completing the job” would entail the following:
> + Process the transactions, build block and broadcast the proposed block + Provide the attestation to the proposed block
> + The block is optimistically considered valid


### Thread part 14 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931455188856950
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 131, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 13)
> + Some other node takes up the work for proposing the next block
> + Now the original node has to generate ZK proof of the validity of the proposed block
> + Next the node will deliver the ZK proof of the block and the attestation of running the proving correctly


### Thread part 15 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931468170227857
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 132, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 14)
> + Node can generate small ZK proofs that do not require a lot of computational power
> + A separate external aggregator can aggregate them into a larger one.
> + Aggregating does not require witnesses


### Thread part 16 — @saxenism — 2025-11-10 17:12 UTC (2025-11-10 18:12 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1987931470426763339
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 128, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 15)
> + Assuming ALL credentials provided by the TEE node (ZK proof and both attestations) check out, the block is finalised

        > Hello everyone, it is with saddened hearts that we announce the shutdown of Bunni.
        >
        > The recent exploit has forced Bunni's growth to a halt, and in order to securely relaunch we'd need to pay 6-7 figures in audit & monitoring expenses alone – requiring capital that we simply don't have. It'd also take months of development & BD effort just to get Bunni back to where it was before the exploit, which we cannot afford. Thus, we have decided it's best to shut down Bunni.
        >
        > Here's what will happen:
        > - Bunni users will still be able to withdraw assets via the Bunni website until further notice.
        > - We intend to distribute the remaining treasury assets to BUNNI, LIT, and veBUNNI holders based on a snapshot. However, the validation of the legal process is ongoing, and the exact details of the distribution will be shared at a later date once the legal process is finalized. Team members will be excluded from the snapshot.
        > - The Bunni v2 smart contracts have been relicensed from BUSL to MIT, enabling everyone to utilize our innovations such as LDFs, surge fees, and autonomous rebalancing. We have pushed the AMM space forward by a generation, and it would be a shame if our efforts went to waste.
        > - We will continue working with law enforcement to recover the stolen funds from the exploiter.
        >
        > Thank you to everyone who has supported us throughout our journey to push DeFi forward.

> Fun tidbit about TEEs.  
>
> For Point of Sale machines, practically, TEEs are required by PCI PTS/DSS standards. Your VISA and Amex cards won't even work in a PoS machine without TEEs.
>
> TEEs are perhaps the only thing in web3 that has been battle-tested outside the industry.

## 2025-10-21 · Post · Yes. For that, we really need to drill down into constant-time coding practices.


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-10-21 07:16 UTC (2025-10-21 08:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1980533607467974798
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1144, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Yes. For that, we really need to drill down into constant-time coding practices.
>
> What that means is implementing cryptographic code so that execution time, memory access and control flow DO NOT depend on secret data.
>
> Eg,
> + No branching like `if(keybit == 1) { ... } else { ... }
> + No array lookups indexed by secret values
> + All operations (multiplication, XOR, modular exponentiation, etc) take identical time and touch identical memory addresses for all possible secrets.
>
> All this would typically imply giving up on a few optimisation tricks, but it's all about how much hindrance you can add in the path of a really motivated adversary.

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/hackerjedi666/status/1979951717744508976

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @hackerjedi666 — 2025-10-19 16:44 UTC (2025-10-19 17:44 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/hackerjedi666/status/1979951717744508976
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1277, like 1, quote 1, reply 0, retweet 0
    
    > True, 
    >
    > Somehow side channel attacks are getting common these days.
    >
    > I once read a blog (wishing i saved it) in which data-dependent code caused bursts of accesses, leaking info about the program's logic or inputs. 
    >
    > It even bypasses ORAM because it exploits the rate of operations, not the addresses lmao.
    >
    > Haven't dabbled much into oram but yeah it was still pretty cool.

    #### Quoted post — @saxenism — 2025-08-29 08:41 UTC (2025-08-29 09:41 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1961348543173755177
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 879, like 1, quote 1, reply 0, retweet 0
    
    > Fucking Hell. I kinda understand now why judging and triaging sucks for everyone involved.
    >
    > I don't have time. The triager doesn't have time. The client doesn't have time. Everyone's BP is going up.
    >
    > How to solve this mess?
    >
    > 1. triager & whitehat get on a video call.
    > 2. call is time-bound. 30 minutes tops.
    > 3. a mutually chosen upon LLM, pre-fed with all required context joins.
    > 4. LLM listens to entire conversation and the pleas made to the LLM specifically from both parties.
    > 5. whoever LLM thinks is right at end of 30 minutes, is the winner
    >
    > ===
    >
    > Irrespective of the outcome:
    >
    > If the judge was right and wins: the drama doesn't drag on.
    > If the judge was right and loses, the client simply doesn't implement the fix
    >
    > If the whitehat was right and loses: he just reports it after the contest in a BBP.
    > If the whitehat was right and wins: he makes bank

## 2025-08-27 · Post · How do you deterministically identify between multiple actors with the same txns, who is the cutesy 


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-08-27 04:44 UTC (2025-08-27 05:44 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1960564127937565130
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 689, like 5, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> How do you deterministically identify between multiple actors with the same txns, who is the cutesy original transaction creator and who are the money-hungry MEV extractooors?
>
> Because if anyone who's txn is included is taxed... then I can make any txn from anyone pretty expensive by spamming those same txns in the mempool.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GzVSPfeWsAA5cHj.png — no alt text provided by author

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/0xnagu/status/1960545936998039653

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @1535531236265181184 — 2025-08-27 03:32 UTC (2025-08-27 04:32 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/1535531236265181184/status/1960545936998039653
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 749, like 3, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 0
    
    > 3/10
    > The Math:
    > An extractor sees an opportunity worth k. We add an app-level tax T(x) = τx on top of their priority fee x.
    >
    > Their profit-maximizing bid becomes x_* = k/(1+τ).
    >
    > The key takeaway: the dApp now captures a  τ/(1+τ) share of the MEV pie.

## 2025-08-22 · Post · A rather elaborate hack/drain attempt on me


Tags (automatic): ZK, AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-08-22 07:38 UTC (2025-08-22 08:38 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1958796012115640471
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 8, impression 4769, like 63, quote 2, reply 12, retweet 4

> A rather elaborate hack/drain attempt on me
>
> I want to share a story with everyone, especially my security researcher friends, about an age-old scam going on, but in a new skin.
>
> So, here's what happened.
>
> 1. I announce that I have left ZKSync's security team in a tweet that garners a bunch of attention online.
>
> 2. A bunch of people follow me from that tweet, including this person, @Zixi_Eth (img0)
>
> 3. 2 days later, I receive a text from him stating that @mirana is looking for a security expert to help them with their security requirements and sends a pretty detailed message about the responsibilities (img1)
>
> 4. I pick a date and time from Zixi's Calendly. Check out @mirana's actual teams page and DO FIND a Zixi Wang listed there (img2). Since it did not have a "Twitter", I grew a bit sus of this account.
>
> 5. The time for our meet comes. I hop over into a Zoom call, he takes a full-fledged interview and asked questions like:
> a) Most exciting bug you ever found
> b) Hardest challenge you solved at your workplace
> c) What inspires you 
> YADAYADAYADA
>
> 6. Then he proceeds to drop THE line that makes every rational decision-making drop dead in its tracks. He said that, according to him, I am the PERFECT FIT for the role, and the pay would be a minimum of $250/hour.
> a) This is a classic rugpull move, where the scammer tries to heighten an emotion (greed, in this case) to make you underthink your next decisions.
>
> 7. He says, "yk what, actually our CEO's on a call right now with the `core operational team`, how about you jump on the call now, and we can finalise your contract today itself.
> a) ANOTHER red flag: "Rushing me"
>
> 8. So, he sends over an invite link to a workspace at jandi[dot]ai. Says it's a Slack alternative popular in China. 
>
> > I am almost out at this point in time, but just play along to see where the actual scam happens.
> > Specifically, the link was: jandi[dot]ai/invite/workspace-2M3QOL
>
> 9. So, I sign into the new work with an email id which was (unfortunately) not a burner email. And I see a meeting happening there, but the bummer is I CANNOT join the meeting on my browser.
>
> > A pop-up claimed that I HAD to install a Mac/Windows client to access this "feature". This was followed by the "Download Now" link ofc.
>
> 10. Zixi, my saviour, texts me on Jandi, asking if something's wrong and offers to help me "set up Jandi" over a Zoom call.
>
> 11. I ask him to kindly fuck off, but he still says, "Don't worry, I'll invite my CEO over a Zoom call if you're not comfortable with Jandi and share your assessment with him in the meantime".
>
> 12. I disconnect and reach out to legitimate employees at @mirana, and they confirm that this handle is, in fact, an imposter (img3)
>
> ====
>
> That's it.
>
> That's the story.
>
> Read it, learn from it and stay safe.
>
> The only reason I am sharing it here on X is that I was able to protect myself this time because I had read about multiple drain (attempt) incidents from a LOT of CT folks. 
>
> They helped me build my invariants for interacting with strangers from CT. 
>
> Huge shout-out to everyone who shared their learnings with the world even after losing money and powering through the embarrassment. You are THE BEST.
>
> Thanks.

- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/Gy8DzYgWcAAUjOa.jpg — no alt text provided by author

## 2025-01-15 · Thread · While Linus's Law ("given enough eyeballs, all bugs are shallow") helped scale Linux, the blockchain


Tags (automatic): Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge


### Post — @saxenism — 2025-01-15 04:10 UTC (2025-01-15 05:10 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1879380630112464998
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 508, like 5, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> While Linus's Law ("given enough eyeballs, all bugs are shallow") helped scale Linux, the blockchain industry needs a more nuanced approach. 
>
> We're not just dealing with software crashes - we're dealing with millions in potential losses.


### Thread part 2 — @saxenism — 2025-01-15 04:10 UTC (2025-01-15 05:10 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1879380633769914531
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 36, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Core blockchain infrastructure powering our industry should absolutely be open sourced. 
>
> But timing matters. 
>
> "Building in public" should NOT be our default. 
>
> Open sourcing shit as you code it, is probably not the best decision as well.


### Thread part 3 — @saxenism — 2025-01-15 04:10 UTC (2025-01-15 05:10 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1879380636194197723
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 27, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Instead, foundations/companies should set clear timelines for open-sourcing different components of their codebase. 
>
> Before the public release, the code should have had multiple professional audits, thorough internal reviews, and rigorous PR review processes.


### Thread part 4 — @saxenism — 2025-01-15 04:10 UTC (2025-01-15 05:10 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1879380639977533467
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 208, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The stakes in blockchain are different. A bug doesn't just mean downtime - it could mean irreversible financial losses. 
>
> We need to balance transparency with security.
>
> Open source remains crucial for trust and innovation, but it needs to be deliberate, not immediate.


### Thread part 5 — @saxenism — 2025-01-15 04:10 UTC (2025-01-15 05:10 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/saxenism/status/1879380642410160427
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 178, like 1, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 0

>
